========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I came in because my head just feels… cloudy, I guess is the best way to say it. I manage properties and there are always board packets, vendor emails, little decisions all day long, and I used to be pretty quick with that kind of thing. Now I’ll be staring at an email about something simple, like approving a vendor or deciding what to tell the board, and I just sit there. It’s like I can read the words but I can’t get my mind to settle on what to do.

Even simple things that I used to handle fast feel harder than they should, and it’s frustrating because I know I’m not usually like that. I keep thinking, “Come on, just decide,” but my brain feels slow and foggy. That’s mainly why I’m here.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because my head just feels… cloudy, I guess is the best way to say it. I manage properties and there are always board packets, vendor emails, little decisions all day long, and I used to be pretty quick with that kind of thing. Now I’ll be staring at an email about something simple, like approving a vendor or deciding what to tell the board, and I just sit there. It’s like I can read the words but I can’t get my mind to settle on what to do.\n\nEven simple things that I used to handle fast feel harder than they should, and it’s frustrating because I know I’m not usually like that. I keep thinking, “Come on, just decide,” but my brain feels slow and foggy. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d call it sad exactly. I mean, I’ve been… off. Low, maybe. It’s hard to put a clean word on it. Since he retired, the house just feels different, and I feel like I can’t quite get my footing. It’s not like I’m sitting there crying all day or anything like that, but I do feel weighed down and not like myself.

I keep trying to tell myself it’s just an adjustment, because of course it’s a big change having someone home all the time after years of having my own routine. But yes, I guess there has been a depressed sort of feeling mixed in with the cloudiness. Not dramatic, just… heavy and frustrating.

Not really, no. That’s actually one of the things that made me think maybe this isn’t just “being tired” or annoyed about the change at home.

I usually go to my dance class, and it’s not like I’m a great dancer or anything, but I like the music and seeing the same people, moving around a little. Lately I just don’t want to go. I think about getting dressed and driving over there and I just… can’t make myself care enough.

And I usually like looking at those little galleries, the small ones, just wandering in and seeing what people have made. That used to clear my head a bit. But these past couple weeks, even that feels like too much effort, like I’d just be standing there pretending to enjoy it.

Even cooking Sunday food for my family, which is usually something that makes me feel like myself, I haven’t wanted to do it. I still think about it, because it’s what I do, but the feeling behind it isn’t there. It’s like the things that usually bring me back to myself just don’t have the same pull right now.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I have. I know I sort of touched on that already, but yes — the things I normally enjoy just haven’t been pulling me in.

Like my dance class, for example. Usually I like going, even if I grumble a little getting ready. Once I’m there, the music and moving around and seeing familiar faces usually makes me feel more like myself. But lately I just don’t want to go at all. It feels like too much to get myself there, and even the thought of it doesn’t give me that little spark.

And I usually enjoy looking at the little galleries, just wandering around and seeing what people are making. That’s normally something quiet that I do for myself. But these past couple weeks, I haven’t wanted to do that either.

Even cooking Sunday food for my family — that’s a big one for me, because it’s usually something that makes me feel useful and connected and like myself. But lately I haven’t wanted to do it. It’s not that I suddenly hate these things. It’s more like the enjoyment has gone flat.

  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I haven’t weighed myself, so I can’t say for sure if the number has changed. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything like that.

But my eating has definitely been off. I’ll have coffee in the morning, and then maybe a few bites of something, and somehow that carries me through most of the day. Not in a healthy way, just like I can’t be bothered or nothing sounds worth making.

Then at night I’ll start picking at things — tortillas, something sweet if it’s around — but I’m not even really enjoying it. It’s more like I’m standing there nibbling because it’s there, not because I’m hungry in a normal way. So I don’t know about weight, but my appetite and eating pattern are not like they usually are.

I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I don’t think I’ve been having some dramatic thing where I’m up all night every night, if that’s what you mean.

Sleep has maybe been a little… unsettled? But I also don’t always keep track well. Some nights I get into bed and I’m tired, and then I’m just lying there longer than I want to be, thinking about small things. Other nights I fall asleep okay enough. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a big clear pattern, though.

I guess what I can say is I haven’t been waking up and thinking, “Oh, I only slept two hours,” or anything like that. It’s more that my sleep doesn’t feel especially refreshing, but I don’t know if that counts as trouble sleeping or just me feeling off in general.

Yes… I have. Not like I think I’m a terrible person all around, exactly, but I keep circling back to this thing with the residents.

I spoke too big, I think. I said we could probably get certain things handled, and I sounded more sure than I should have before I really checked the money properly. And now I keep thinking, “Why did I do that? Why didn’t I slow down and look at the numbers first?” Nobody has come to me and said I ruined anything, and no one has accused me of anything like that, but in my head it feels like I let them down.

It’s embarrassing to even say, because maybe it’s not as big as I’m making it. But I keep replaying it, like I promised more than I should have, and now I feel foolish and guilty about it. Like people trusted me to know better, and I should have been more careful.

Like people trusted me to know better, and I should have been more careful.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had any thoughts about death or dying over the past two weeks?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 64 / property_real_estate_or_community_association_manager / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Death_Suicide\nmanifestation: \"A few mornings I have had the scary thought that maybe it would be easier if I just did not wake up, but I do not want to hurt myself and I have no plan.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I… yes, a little, and it scares me to say that out loud.

It hasn’t been like I’m planning something, or that I want to hurt myself. I don’t. I really don’t have a plan or anything like that. But a few mornings, when I first wake up and I’m still lying there, I’ve had this thought like… maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Like if I could just not have to face the day.

And then I get scared of the thought, because I don’t want to die, not exactly. It’s more like I feel worn down in that moment, and my mind goes to that place before I can stop it. But I haven’t done anything to hurt myself, and I’m not thinking about a way to do it. It’s just been that thought, a few times, and it upset me.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think both, which sounds odd when I say it.

At home, especially if I’m just going from one room to another or trying to get myself started on something, I feel slow. Like my legs are heavy, almost like I’m dragging myself around the house. It’s not that I can’t move, but everything takes more pushing than it should. I’ll stand there a second before doing something simple, like going to pick something up, and it feels like my body is behind me.

But then if I’m in a meeting, it’s different. I notice I’m tapping my pen, or moving in the chair, crossing and uncrossing my legs, shifting around. I’ll feel too keyed up to just sit still and listen normally. I don’t know if other people notice it as much as I do, but I notice it, and then I get self-conscious about it, which probably makes me move around more.

So yes, at home I feel slowed down, like everything is heavy, and around other people, especially in meetings, I can feel kind of wound up in my body.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’d say it’s more mild than a complete breakdown, because I am still getting myself to work. I’m not just staying home or disappearing on people. But it is interfering, yes.

The main thing is I don’t trust my judgment the way I usually do. So decisions that I would normally just make, I’m slowing them down. I’ll sit with them longer, or I’ll say, “Let me look at that again,” even when maybe it doesn’t need that much looking. And I’ve been asking my assistant to double-check vendor details more than usual—things like whether I understood the price right, or if the paperwork matches what I thought it said. I’m grateful she can do that, but it makes me feel a little embarrassed, like I should be able to handle it without leaning on her so much.

And I’ve put off a couple of board items, not because they’re impossible, but because I just don’t feel confident enough in my own thinking right now. I keep worrying I’ll miss something or say something too firmly and then regret it. So yes, I’m functioning, but I’m doing everything more carefully, more slowly, and I’m relying on other people to check me. It’s not ruining everything, but it’s definitely getting in the way.

It’s more like I’m standing there nibbling because it’s there, not because I’m hungry in a normal way. I still think about it, because it’s what I do, but the feeling behind it isn’t there. It’s like the things that usually bring me back to myself just don’t have the same pull right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any loss of interest in activities that you usually find pleasurable over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. I know I sort of touched on that already, but yes — the things I normally enjoy just haven’t been pulling me in.\n\nLike my dance class, for example. Usually I like going, even if I grumble a little getting ready. Once I’m there, the music and moving around and seeing familiar faces usually makes me feel more like myself. But lately I just don’t want to go at all. It feels like too much to get myself there, and even the thought of it doesn’t give me that little spark.\n\nAnd I usually enjoy looking at the little galleries, just wandering around and seeing what people are making. That’s normally something quiet that I do for myself. But these past couple weeks, I haven’t wanted to do that either.\n\nEven cooking Sunday food for my family — that’s a big one for me, because it’s usually something that makes me feel useful and connected and like myself. But lately I haven’t wanted to do it. It’s not that I suddenly hate these things. It’s more like the enjoyment has gone flat.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I haven’t weighed myself, so I can’t say for sure if the number has changed. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything like that.\n\nBut my eating has definitely been off. I’ll have coffee in the morning, and then maybe a few bites of something, and somehow that carries me through most of the day. Not in a healthy way, just like I can’t be bothered or nothing sounds worth making.\n\nThen at night I’ll start picking at things — tortillas, something sweet if it’s around — but I’m not even really enjoying it. It’s more like I’m standing there nibbling because it’s there, not because I’m hungry in a normal way. So I don’t know about weight, but my appetite and eating pattern are not like they usually are.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any trouble sleeping over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I don’t think I’ve been having some dramatic thing where I’m up all night every night, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSleep has maybe been a little… unsettled? But I also don’t always keep track well. Some nights I get into bed and I’m tired, and then I’m just lying there longer than I want to be, thinking about small things. Other nights I fall asleep okay enough. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a big clear pattern, though.\n\nI guess what I can say is I haven’t been waking up and thinking, “Oh, I only slept two hours,” or anything like that. It’s more that my sleep doesn’t feel especially refreshing, but I don’t know if that counts as trouble sleeping or just me feeling off in general.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or worthless about anything in particular over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. Not like I think I’m a terrible person all around, exactly, but I keep circling back to this thing with the residents.\n\nI spoke too big, I think. I said we could probably get certain things handled, and I sounded more sure than I should have before I really checked the money properly. And now I keep thinking, “Why did I do that? Why didn’t I slow down and look at the numbers first?” Nobody has come to me and said I ruined anything, and no one has accused me of anything like that, but in my head it feels like I let them down.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to even say, because maybe it’s not as big as I’m making it. But I keep replaying it, like I promised more than I should have, and now I feel foolish and guilty about it. Like people trusted me to know better, and I should have been more careful.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about death or dying over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I… yes, a little, and it scares me to say that out loud.\n\nIt hasn’t been like I’m planning something, or that I want to hurt myself. I don’t. I really don’t have a plan or anything like that. But a few mornings, when I first wake up and I’m still lying there, I’ve had this thought like… maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Like if I could just not have to face the day.\n\nAnd then I get scared of the thought, because I don’t want to die, not exactly. It’s more like I feel worn down in that moment, and my mind goes to that place before I can stop it. But I haven’t done anything to hurt myself, and I’m not thinking about a way to do it. It’s just been that thought, a few times, and it upset me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like you're speeding up in your thoughts or actions over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think both, which sounds odd when I say it.\n\nAt home, especially if I’m just going from one room to another or trying to get myself started on something, I feel slow. Like my legs are heavy, almost like I’m dragging myself around the house. It’s not that I can’t move, but everything takes more pushing than it should. I’ll stand there a second before doing something simple, like going to pick something up, and it feels like my body is behind me.\n\nBut then if I’m in a meeting, it’s different. I notice I’m tapping my pen, or moving in the chair, crossing and uncrossing my legs, shifting around. I’ll feel too keyed up to just sit still and listen normally. I don’t know if other people notice it as much as I do, but I notice it, and then I get self-conscious about it, which probably makes me move around more.\n\nSo yes, at home I feel slowed down, like everything is heavy, and around other people, especially in meetings, I can feel kind of wound up in my body.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your low, cloudy feeling and lack of interest in activities are interfering with your daily life and relationships a lot, or more mildly?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more mild than a complete breakdown, because I am still getting myself to work. I’m not just staying home or disappearing on people. But it is interfering, yes.\n\nThe main thing is I don’t trust my judgment the way I usually do. So decisions that I would normally just make, I’m slowing them down. I’ll sit with them longer, or I’ll say, “Let me look at that again,” even when maybe it doesn’t need that much looking. And I’ve been asking my assistant to double-check vendor details more than usual—things like whether I understood the price right, or if the paperwork matches what I thought it said. I’m grateful she can do that, but it makes me feel a little embarrassed, like I should be able to handle it without leaning on her so much.\n\nAnd I’ve put off a couple of board items, not because they’re impossible, but because I just don’t feel confident enough in my own thinking right now. I keep worrying I’ll miss something or say something too firmly and then regret it. So yes, I’m functioning, but I’m doing everything more carefully, more slowly, and I’m relying on other people to check me. It’s not ruining everything, but it’s definitely getting in the way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes… I mean, significantly might be a strong word if you mean I’m unable to function, because I am still getting up and going to work. I’m showing up. I’m not letting everything fall apart.

But it is affecting me, more than I’d like to admit. I feel like I don’t trust my judgment right now, so I’m slowing decisions down that I would normally just make and move on from. I keep second-guessing myself, especially with work things. I’ve had my assistant double-check vendor details more than usual, like prices or paperwork, because I’m worried I’ll miss something obvious or misunderstand something.

And I’ve put off a couple of board items too, not because I don’t care about them, but because I just don’t feel steady enough in my thinking to handle them the way I normally would. So I’m functioning, yes, but it’s taking more effort, and I’m relying on extra checking and delaying things. That’s not really like me.

That’s actually one of the things that made me think maybe this isn’t just “being tired” or annoyed about the change at home.\n\nI usually go to my dance class, and it’s not like I’m a great dancer or anything, but I like the music and seeing the same people, moving around a little. Lately I just don’t want to go. But lately I just don’t want to go at all. It feels like too much to get myself there, and even the thought of it doesn’t give me that little spark.\n\nAnd I usually enjoy looking at the little galleries, just wandering around and seeing what people are making. That’s normally something quiet that I do for myself. But these past couple weeks, I haven’t wanted to do that either.\n\nEven cooking Sunday food for my family — that’s a big one for me, because it’s usually something that makes me feel useful and connected and like myself. But lately I haven’t wanted to do it. It’s not that I suddenly hate these things. It’s more like the enjoyment has gone flat.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I haven’t weighed myself, so I can’t say for sure if the number has changed. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything like that.\n\nBut my eating has definitely been off. I’ll have coffee in the morning, and then maybe a few bites of something, and somehow that carries me through most of the day. Not in a healthy way, just like I can’t be bothered or nothing sounds worth making.\n\nThen at night I’ll start picking at things — tortillas, something sweet if it’s around — but I’m not even really enjoying it. It’s more like I’m standing there nibbling because it’s there, not because I’m hungry in a normal way. So I don’t know about weight, but my appetite and eating pattern are not like they usually are.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any trouble sleeping over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I don’t think I’ve been having some dramatic thing where I’m up all night every night, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSleep has maybe been a little… unsettled? But I also don’t always keep track well. Some nights I get into bed and I’m tired, and then I’m just lying there longer than I want to be, thinking about small things. Other nights I fall asleep okay enough. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a big clear pattern, though.\n\nI guess what I can say is I haven’t been waking up and thinking, “Oh, I only slept two hours,” or anything like that. It’s more that my sleep doesn’t feel especially refreshing, but I don’t know if that counts as trouble sleeping or just me feeling off in general.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or worthless about anything in particular over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. Not like I think I’m a terrible person all around, exactly, but I keep circling back to this thing with the residents.\n\nI spoke too big, I think. I said we could probably get certain things handled, and I sounded more sure than I should have before I really checked the money properly. And now I keep thinking, “Why did I do that? Why didn’t I slow down and look at the numbers first?” Nobody has come to me and said I ruined anything, and no one has accused me of anything like that, but in my head it feels like I let them down.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to even say, because maybe it’s not as big as I’m making it. But I keep replaying it, like I promised more than I should have, and now I feel foolish and guilty about it. Like people trusted me to know better, and I should have been more careful.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about death or dying over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I… yes, a little, and it scares me to say that out loud.\n\nIt hasn’t been like I’m planning something, or that I want to hurt myself. I don’t. I really don’t have a plan or anything like that. But a few mornings, when I first wake up and I’m still lying there, I’ve had this thought like… maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Like if I could just not have to face the day.\n\nAnd then I get scared of the thought, because I don’t want to die, not exactly. It’s more like I feel worn down in that moment, and my mind goes to that place before I can stop it. But I haven’t done anything to hurt myself, and I’m not thinking about a way to do it. It’s just been that thought, a few times, and it upset me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like you're speeding up in your thoughts or actions over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think both, which sounds odd when I say it.\n\nAt home, especially if I’m just going from one room to another or trying to get myself started on something, I feel slow. Like my legs are heavy, almost like I’m dragging myself around the house. It’s not that I can’t move, but everything takes more pushing than it should. I’ll stand there a second before doing something simple, like going to pick something up, and it feels like my body is behind me.\n\nBut then if I’m in a meeting, it’s different. I notice I’m tapping my pen, or moving in the chair, crossing and uncrossing my legs, shifting around. I’ll feel too keyed up to just sit still and listen normally. I don’t know if other people notice it as much as I do, but I notice it, and then I get self-conscious about it, which probably makes me move around more.\n\nSo yes, at home I feel slowed down, like everything is heavy, and around other people, especially in meetings, I can feel kind of wound up in my body.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your low, cloudy feeling and lack of interest in activities are interfering with your daily life and relationships a lot, or more mildly?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more mild than a complete breakdown, because I am still getting myself to work. I’m not just staying home or disappearing on people. But it is interfering, yes.\n\nThe main thing is I don’t trust my judgment the way I usually do. So decisions that I would normally just make, I’m slowing them down. I’ll sit with them longer, or I’ll say, “Let me look at that again,” even when maybe it doesn’t need that much looking. And I’ve been asking my assistant to double-check vendor details more than usual—things like whether I understood the price right, or if the paperwork matches what I thought it said. I’m grateful she can do that, but it makes me feel a little embarrassed, like I should be able to handle it without leaning on her so much.\n\nAnd I’ve put off a couple of board items, not because they’re impossible, but because I just don’t feel confident enough in my own thinking right now. I keep worrying I’ll miss something or say something too firmly and then regret it. So yes, I’m functioning, but I’m doing everything more carefully, more slowly, and I’m relying on other people to check me. It’s not ruining everything, but it’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your current feelings of being low and cloudy are significantly impacting your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, significantly might be a strong word if you mean I’m unable to function, because I am still getting up and going to work. I’m showing up. I’m not letting everything fall apart.\n\nBut it is affecting me, more than I’d like to admit. I feel like I don’t trust my judgment right now, so I’m slowing decisions down that I would normally just make and move on from. I keep second-guessing myself, especially with work things. I’ve had my assistant double-check vendor details more than usual, like prices or paperwork, because I’m worried I’ll miss something obvious or misunderstand something.\n\nAnd I’ve put off a couple of board items too, not because I don’t care about them, but because I just don’t feel steady enough in my thinking to handle them the way I normally would. So I’m functioning, yes, but it’s taking more effort, and I’m relying on extra checking and delaying things. That’s not really like me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Bipolar II Disorder, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like you're speeding up in your thoughts or actions over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think both, which sounds odd when I say it.\n\nAt home, especially if I’m just going from one room to another or trying to get myself started on something, I feel slow. Like my legs are heavy, almost like I’m dragging myself around the house. It’s not that I can’t move, but everything takes more pushing than it should. I’ll stand there a second before doing something simple, like going to pick something up, and it feels like my body is behind me.\n\nBut then if I’m in a meeting, it’s different. I notice I’m tapping my pen, or moving in the chair, crossing and uncrossing my legs, shifting around. I’ll feel too keyed up to just sit still and listen normally. I don’t know if other people notice it as much as I do, but I notice it, and then I get self-conscious about it, which probably makes me move around more.\n\nSo yes, at home I feel slowed down, like everything is heavy, and around other people, especially in meetings, I can feel kind of wound up in my body.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your low, cloudy feeling and lack of interest in activities are interfering with your daily life and relationships a lot, or more mildly?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more mild than a complete breakdown, because I am still getting myself to work. I’m not just staying home or disappearing on people. But it is interfering, yes.\n\nThe main thing is I don’t trust my judgment the way I usually do. So decisions that I would normally just make, I’m slowing them down. I’ll sit with them longer, or I’ll say, “Let me look at that again,” even when maybe it doesn’t need that much looking. And I’ve been asking my assistant to double-check vendor details more than usual—things like whether I understood the price right, or if the paperwork matches what I thought it said. I’m grateful she can do that, but it makes me feel a little embarrassed, like I should be able to handle it without leaning on her so much.\n\nAnd I’ve put off a couple of board items, not because they’re impossible, but because I just don’t feel confident enough in my own thinking right now. I keep worrying I’ll miss something or say something too firmly and then regret it. So yes, I’m functioning, but I’m doing everything more carefully, more slowly, and I’m relying on other people to check me. It’s not ruining everything, but it’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your current feelings of being low and cloudy are significantly impacting your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, significantly might be a strong word if you mean I’m unable to function, because I am still getting up and going to work. I’m showing up. I’m not letting everything fall apart.\n\nBut it is affecting me, more than I’d like to admit. I feel like I don’t trust my judgment right now, so I’m slowing decisions down that I would normally just make and move on from. I keep second-guessing myself, especially with work things. I’ve had my assistant double-check vendor details more than usual, like prices or paperwork, because I’m worried I’ll miss something obvious or misunderstand something.\n\nAnd I’ve put off a couple of board items too, not because I don’t care about them, but because I just don’t feel steady enough in my thinking to handle them the way I normally would. So I’m functioning, yes, but it’s taking more effort, and I’m relying on extra checking and delaying things. That’s not really like me.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say that your current low and cloudy feelings are affecting your relationships with family and friends?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 64 / property_real_estate_or_community_association_manager / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I am still getting to work, but I have had to slow down decisions, ask my assistant to double-check vendor details, and put off a couple of board items because I do not trust my judgment right now.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
With family and friends… I guess a little, but it’s not as obvious there as it is at work.

I’m still going to work, so from the outside I probably look like I’m managing. But because I don’t trust my judgment right now, I’ve been slower about decisions, and that spills over into how I am with people. Like I’m less quick to say, “Yes, let’s do that,” or “No, that’s not a good idea.” I hesitate more. I second-guess myself.

At work it’s clearer because I’ve been asking my assistant to double-check vendor details, and I’ve put off a couple of board items because I just don’t feel confident in my thinking. With family or friends, it’s more that I’m not as decisive or as present, maybe. I don’t think I’m fighting with anyone or anything like that, but I’m quieter, more careful. I don’t want to say the wrong thing or make a decision and then regret it.

So yes, it affects them some, but I’d call it mild. I’m not cut off from people, but I’m not quite myself with them either.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because my head just feels… cloudy, I guess is the best way to say it. I manage properties and there are always board packets, vendor emails, little decisions all day long, and I used to be pretty quick with that kind of thing. Now I’ll be staring at an email about something simple, like approving a vendor or deciding what to tell the board, and I just sit there. It’s like I can read the words but I can’t get my mind to settle on what to do.\n\nEven simple things that I used to handle fast feel harder than they should, and it’s frustrating because I know I’m not usually like that. I keep thinking, “Come on, just decide,” but my brain feels slow and foggy. That’s mainly why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this cloudy feeling and difficulty making decisions?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two weeks now, I think. It sort of came on pretty sharply around when my husband retired, because all of a sudden he was home all day, every day, and the whole rhythm of the house changed.\n\nBefore that I was getting through things more like myself, but then there was this change in my energy and my confidence, and then it kind of dropped into this low, cloudy feeling. So… yes, about two weeks, not months and months, but these last couple weeks have felt much longer than they sound.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed since your husband retired and your household rhythm changed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad exactly. I mean, I’ve been… off. Low, maybe. It’s hard to put a clean word on it. Since he retired, the house just feels different, and I feel like I can’t quite get my footing. It’s not like I’m sitting there crying all day or anything like that, but I do feel weighed down and not like myself.\n\nI keep trying to tell myself it’s just an adjustment, because of course it’s a big change having someone home all the time after years of having my own routine. But yes, I guess there has been a depressed sort of feeling mixed in with the cloudiness. Not dramatic, just… heavy and frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things you usually like to do over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. That’s actually one of the things that made me think maybe this isn’t just “being tired” or annoyed about the change at home.\n\nI usually go to my dance class, and it’s not like I’m a great dancer or anything, but I like the music and seeing the same people, moving around a little. Lately I just don’t want to go. I think about getting dressed and driving over there and I just… can’t make myself care enough.\n\nAnd I usually like looking at those little galleries, the small ones, just wandering in and seeing what people have made. That used to clear my head a bit. But these past couple weeks, even that feels like too much effort, like I’d just be standing there pretending to enjoy it.\n\nEven cooking Sunday food for my family, which is usually something that makes me feel like myself, I haven’t wanted to do it. I still think about it, because it’s what I do, but the feeling behind it isn’t there. It’s like the things that usually bring me back to myself just don’t have the same pull right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any loss of interest in activities that you usually find pleasurable over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. I know I sort of touched on that already, but yes — the things I normally enjoy just haven’t been pulling me in.\n\nLike my dance class, for example. Usually I like going, even if I grumble a little getting ready. Once I’m there, the music and moving around and seeing familiar faces usually makes me feel more like myself. But lately I just don’t want to go at all. It feels like too much to get myself there, and even the thought of it doesn’t give me that little spark.\n\nAnd I usually enjoy looking at the little galleries, just wandering around and seeing what people are making. That’s normally something quiet that I do for myself. But these past couple weeks, I haven’t wanted to do that either.\n\nEven cooking Sunday food for my family — that’s a big one for me, because it’s usually something that makes me feel useful and connected and like myself. But lately I haven’t wanted to do it. It’s not that I suddenly hate these things. It’s more like the enjoyment has gone flat.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I haven’t weighed myself, so I can’t say for sure if the number has changed. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything like that.\n\nBut my eating has definitely been off. I’ll have coffee in the morning, and then maybe a few bites of something, and somehow that carries me through most of the day. Not in a healthy way, just like I can’t be bothered or nothing sounds worth making.\n\nThen at night I’ll start picking at things — tortillas, something sweet if it’s around — but I’m not even really enjoying it. It’s more like I’m standing there nibbling because it’s there, not because I’m hungry in a normal way. So I don’t know about weight, but my appetite and eating pattern are not like they usually are.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any trouble sleeping over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I don’t think I’ve been having some dramatic thing where I’m up all night every night, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSleep has maybe been a little… unsettled? But I also don’t always keep track well. Some nights I get into bed and I’m tired, and then I’m just lying there longer than I want to be, thinking about small things. Other nights I fall asleep okay enough. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a big clear pattern, though.\n\nI guess what I can say is I haven’t been waking up and thinking, “Oh, I only slept two hours,” or anything like that. It’s more that my sleep doesn’t feel especially refreshing, but I don’t know if that counts as trouble sleeping or just me feeling off in general.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or worthless about anything in particular over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. Not like I think I’m a terrible person all around, exactly, but I keep circling back to this thing with the residents.\n\nI spoke too big, I think. I said we could probably get certain things handled, and I sounded more sure than I should have before I really checked the money properly. And now I keep thinking, “Why did I do that? Why didn’t I slow down and look at the numbers first?” Nobody has come to me and said I ruined anything, and no one has accused me of anything like that, but in my head it feels like I let them down.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to even say, because maybe it’s not as big as I’m making it. But I keep replaying it, like I promised more than I should have, and now I feel foolish and guilty about it. Like people trusted me to know better, and I should have been more careful.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about death or dying over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I… yes, a little, and it scares me to say that out loud.\n\nIt hasn’t been like I’m planning something, or that I want to hurt myself. I don’t. I really don’t have a plan or anything like that. But a few mornings, when I first wake up and I’m still lying there, I’ve had this thought like… maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Like if I could just not have to face the day.\n\nAnd then I get scared of the thought, because I don’t want to die, not exactly. It’s more like I feel worn down in that moment, and my mind goes to that place before I can stop it. But I haven’t done anything to hurt myself, and I’m not thinking about a way to do it. It’s just been that thought, a few times, and it upset me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like you're speeding up in your thoughts or actions over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think both, which sounds odd when I say it.\n\nAt home, especially if I’m just going from one room to another or trying to get myself started on something, I feel slow. Like my legs are heavy, almost like I’m dragging myself around the house. It’s not that I can’t move, but everything takes more pushing than it should. I’ll stand there a second before doing something simple, like going to pick something up, and it feels like my body is behind me.\n\nBut then if I’m in a meeting, it’s different. I notice I’m tapping my pen, or moving in the chair, crossing and uncrossing my legs, shifting around. I’ll feel too keyed up to just sit still and listen normally. I don’t know if other people notice it as much as I do, but I notice it, and then I get self-conscious about it, which probably makes me move around more.\n\nSo yes, at home I feel slowed down, like everything is heavy, and around other people, especially in meetings, I can feel kind of wound up in my body.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your low, cloudy feeling and lack of interest in activities are interfering with your daily life and relationships a lot, or more mildly?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more mild than a complete breakdown, because I am still getting myself to work. I’m not just staying home or disappearing on people. But it is interfering, yes.\n\nThe main thing is I don’t trust my judgment the way I usually do. So decisions that I would normally just make, I’m slowing them down. I’ll sit with them longer, or I’ll say, “Let me look at that again,” even when maybe it doesn’t need that much looking. And I’ve been asking my assistant to double-check vendor details more than usual—things like whether I understood the price right, or if the paperwork matches what I thought it said. I’m grateful she can do that, but it makes me feel a little embarrassed, like I should be able to handle it without leaning on her so much.\n\nAnd I’ve put off a couple of board items, not because they’re impossible, but because I just don’t feel confident enough in my own thinking right now. I keep worrying I’ll miss something or say something too firmly and then regret it. So yes, I’m functioning, but I’m doing everything more carefully, more slowly, and I’m relying on other people to check me. It’s not ruining everything, but it’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your current feelings of being low and cloudy are significantly impacting your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, significantly might be a strong word if you mean I’m unable to function, because I am still getting up and going to work. I’m showing up. I’m not letting everything fall apart.\n\nBut it is affecting me, more than I’d like to admit. I feel like I don’t trust my judgment right now, so I’m slowing decisions down that I would normally just make and move on from. I keep second-guessing myself, especially with work things. I’ve had my assistant double-check vendor details more than usual, like prices or paperwork, because I’m worried I’ll miss something obvious or misunderstand something.\n\nAnd I’ve put off a couple of board items too, not because I don’t care about them, but because I just don’t feel steady enough in my thinking to handle them the way I normally would. So I’m functioning, yes, but it’s taking more effort, and I’m relying on extra checking and delaying things. That’s not really like me.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your current low and cloudy feelings are affecting your relationships with family and friends?\n\nPatient: With family and friends… I guess a little, but it’s not as obvious there as it is at work.\n\nI’m still going to work, so from the outside I probably look like I’m managing. But because I don’t trust my judgment right now, I’ve been slower about decisions, and that spills over into how I am with people. Like I’m less quick to say, “Yes, let’s do that,” or “No, that’s not a good idea.” I hesitate more. I don’t know if other people notice it as much as I do, but I notice it, and then I get self-conscious about it, which probably makes me move around more.\n\nSo yes, at home I feel slowed down, like everything is heavy, and around other people, especially in meetings, I can feel kind of wound up in my body.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your low, cloudy feeling and lack of interest in activities are interfering with your daily life and relationships a lot, or more mildly?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more mild than a complete breakdown, because I am still getting myself to work. I’m not just staying home or disappearing on people. But it is interfering, yes.\n\nThe main thing is I don’t trust my judgment the way I usually do. So decisions that I would normally just make, I’m slowing them down. I’ll sit with them longer, or I’ll say, “Let me look at that again,” even when maybe it doesn’t need that much looking. And I’ve been asking my assistant to double-check vendor details more than usual—things like whether I understood the price right, or if the paperwork matches what I thought it said. I’m grateful she can do that, but it makes me feel a little embarrassed, like I should be able to handle it without leaning on her so much.\n\nAnd I’ve put off a couple of board items, not because they’re impossible, but because I just don’t feel confident enough in my own thinking right now. I keep worrying I’ll miss something or say something too firmly and then regret it. So yes, I’m functioning, but I’m doing everything more carefully, more slowly, and I’m relying on other people to check me. It’s not ruining everything, but it’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your current feelings of being low and cloudy are significantly impacting your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, significantly might be a strong word if you mean I’m unable to function, because I am still getting up and going to work. I’m showing up. I’m not letting everything fall apart.\n\nBut it is affecting me, more than I’d like to admit. I feel like I don’t trust my judgment right now, so I’m slowing decisions down that I would normally just make and move on from. I keep second-guessing myself, especially with work things. I’ve had my assistant double-check vendor details more than usual, like prices or paperwork, because I’m worried I’ll miss something obvious or misunderstand something.\n\nAnd I’ve put off a couple of board items too, not because I don’t care about them, but because I just don’t feel steady enough in my thinking to handle them the way I normally would. So I’m functioning, yes, but it’s taking more effort, and I’m relying on extra checking and delaying things. That’s not really like me.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your current low and cloudy feelings are affecting your relationships with family and friends?\n\nPatient: With family and friends… I guess a little, but it’s not as obvious there as it is at work.\n\nI’m still going to work, so from the outside I probably look like I’m managing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think that’s a fair way to put it. Maybe “almost all” sounds a little big, but honestly, the things that usually make me feel like myself, I haven’t wanted to do them.

Like my dance class — normally I love that. Even if I’m tired, once I get there and the music starts, I feel better, I feel like I’m back in my body. Lately I just don’t want to go. I’ll think about getting dressed for it and it feels like too much, or like there’s no point.

And I usually like looking at the little galleries, just wandering around and seeing what people are making. That used to lift me up. Now I think about it and I just feel flat, like I’d be going through the motions.

Even cooking Sunday food for my family… that’s a big one for me. I’ve always liked doing that, planning the food, making enough for everyone, having the house smell right. Lately I don’t have the interest in it. It feels more like a chore than something warm.

So yes, I’m still doing some things, but the pleasure isn’t there the way it usually is. Those are the things that normally tell me I’m okay, and right now I don’t really want them.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because my head just feels… cloudy, I guess is the best way to say it. I manage properties and there are always board packets, vendor emails, little decisions all day long, and I used to be pretty quick with that kind of thing. Now I’ll be staring at an email about something simple, like approving a vendor or deciding what to tell the board, and I just sit there. It’s like I can read the words but I can’t get my mind to settle on what to do.\n\nEven simple things that I used to handle fast feel harder than they should, and it’s frustrating because I know I’m not usually like that. I keep thinking, “Come on, just decide,” but my brain feels slow and foggy. That’s mainly why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this cloudy feeling and difficulty making decisions?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two weeks now, I think. It sort of came on pretty sharply around when my husband retired, because all of a sudden he was home all day, every day, and the whole rhythm of the house changed.\n\nBefore that I was getting through things more like myself, but then there was this change in my energy and my confidence, and then it kind of dropped into this low, cloudy feeling. So… yes, about two weeks, not months and months, but these last couple weeks have felt much longer than they sound.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed since your husband retired and your household rhythm changed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad exactly. I mean, I’ve been… off. Low, maybe. It’s hard to put a clean word on it. Since he retired, the house just feels different, and I feel like I can’t quite get my footing. It’s not like I’m sitting there crying all day or anything like that, but I do feel weighed down and not like myself.\n\nI keep trying to tell myself it’s just an adjustment, because of course it’s a big change having someone home all the time after years of having my own routine. But yes, I guess there has been a depressed sort of feeling mixed in with the cloudiness. Not dramatic, just… heavy and frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things you usually like to do over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. That’s actually one of the things that made me think maybe this isn’t just “being tired” or annoyed about the change at home.\n\nI usually go to my dance class, and it’s not like I’m a great dancer or anything, but I like the music and seeing the same people, moving around a little. Lately I just don’t want to go. I think about getting dressed and driving over there and I just… can’t make myself care enough.\n\nAnd I usually like looking at those little galleries, the small ones, just wandering in and seeing what people have made. That used to clear my head a bit. But these past couple weeks, even that feels like too much effort, like I’d just be standing there pretending to enjoy it.\n\nEven cooking Sunday food for my family, which is usually something that makes me feel like myself, I haven’t wanted to do it. I still think about it, because it’s what I do, but the feeling behind it isn’t there. It’s like the things that usually bring me back to myself just don’t have the same pull right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any loss of interest in activities that you usually find pleasurable over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. I know I sort of touched on that already, but yes — the things I normally enjoy just haven’t been pulling me in.\n\nLike my dance class, for example. Usually I like going, even if I grumble a little getting ready. Once I’m there, the music and moving around and seeing familiar faces usually makes me feel more like myself. But lately I just don’t want to go at all. It feels like too much to get myself there, and even the thought of it doesn’t give me that little spark.\n\nAnd I usually enjoy looking at the little galleries, just wandering around and seeing what people are making. That’s normally something quiet that I do for myself. But these past couple weeks, I haven’t wanted to do that either.\n\nEven cooking Sunday food for my family — that’s a big one for me, because it’s usually something that makes me feel useful and connected and like myself. But lately I haven’t wanted to do it. It’s not that I suddenly hate these things. It’s more like the enjoyment has gone flat.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I haven’t weighed myself, so I can’t say for sure if the number has changed. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything like that.\n\nBut my eating has definitely been off. I’ll have coffee in the morning, and then maybe a few bites of something, and somehow that carries me through most of the day. Not in a healthy way, just like I can’t be bothered or nothing sounds worth making.\n\nThen at night I’ll start picking at things — tortillas, something sweet if it’s around — but I’m not even really enjoying it. It’s more like I’m standing there nibbling because it’s there, not because I’m hungry in a normal way. So I don’t know about weight, but my appetite and eating pattern are not like they usually are.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any trouble sleeping over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I don’t think I’ve been having some dramatic thing where I’m up all night every night, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSleep has maybe been a little… unsettled? But I also don’t always keep track well. Some nights I get into bed and I’m tired, and then I’m just lying there longer than I want to be, thinking about small things. Other nights I fall asleep okay enough. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a big clear pattern, though.\n\nI guess what I can say is I haven’t been waking up and thinking, “Oh, I only slept two hours,” or anything like that. It’s more that my sleep doesn’t feel especially refreshing, but I don’t know if that counts as trouble sleeping or just me feeling off in general.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or worthless about anything in particular over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. Not like I think I’m a terrible person all around, exactly, but I keep circling back to this thing with the residents.\n\nI spoke too big, I think. I said we could probably get certain things handled, and I sounded more sure than I should have before I really checked the money properly. And now I keep thinking, “Why did I do that? Why didn’t I slow down and look at the numbers first?” Nobody has come to me and said I ruined anything, and no one has accused me of anything like that, but in my head it feels like I let them down.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to even say, because maybe it’s not as big as I’m making it. But I keep replaying it, like I promised more than I should have, and now I feel foolish and guilty about it. Like people trusted me to know better, and I should have been more careful.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about death or dying over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I… yes, a little, and it scares me to say that out loud.\n\nIt hasn’t been like I’m planning something, or that I want to hurt myself. I don’t. I really don’t have a plan or anything like that. But a few mornings, when I first wake up and I’m still lying there, I’ve had this thought like… maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Like if I could just not have to face the day.\n\nAnd then I get scared of the thought, because I don’t want to die, not exactly. It’s more like I feel worn down in that moment, and my mind goes to that place before I can stop it. But I haven’t done anything to hurt myself, and I’m not thinking about a way to do it. It’s just been that thought, a few times, and it upset me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like you're speeding up in your thoughts or actions over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think both, which sounds odd when I say it.\n\nAt home, especially if I’m just going from one room to another or trying to get myself started on something, I feel slow. Like my legs are heavy, almost like I’m dragging myself around the house. It’s not that I can’t move, but everything takes more pushing than it should. I’ll stand there a second before doing something simple, like going to pick something up, and it feels like my body is behind me.\n\nBut then if I’m in a meeting, it’s different. I notice I’m tapping my pen, or moving in the chair, crossing and uncrossing my legs, shifting around. I’ll feel too keyed up to just sit still and listen normally. I don’t know if other people notice it as much as I do, but I notice it, and then I get self-conscious about it, which probably makes me move around more.\n\nSo yes, at home I feel slowed down, like everything is heavy, and around other people, especially in meetings, I can feel kind of wound up in my body.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your low, cloudy feeling and lack of interest in activities are interfering with your daily life and relationships a lot, or more mildly?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more mild than a complete breakdown, because I am still getting myself to work. I’m not just staying home or disappearing on people. But it is interfering, yes.\n\nThe main thing is I don’t trust my judgment the way I usually do. So decisions that I would normally just make, I’m slowing them down. I’ll sit with them longer, or I’ll say, “Let me look at that again,” even when maybe it doesn’t need that much looking. And I’ve been asking my assistant to double-check vendor details more than usual—things like whether I understood the price right, or if the paperwork matches what I thought it said. I’m grateful she can do that, but it makes me feel a little embarrassed, like I should be able to handle it without leaning on her so much.\n\nAnd I’ve put off a couple of board items, not because they’re impossible, but because I just don’t feel confident enough in my own thinking right now. I keep worrying I’ll miss something or say something too firmly and then regret it. So yes, I’m functioning, but I’m doing everything more carefully, more slowly, and I’m relying on other people to check me. It’s not ruining everything, but it’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your current feelings of being low and cloudy are significantly impacting your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, significantly might be a strong word if you mean I’m unable to function, because I am still getting up and going to work. I’m showing up. I’m not letting everything fall apart.\n\nBut it is affecting me, more than I’d like to admit. I feel like I don’t trust my judgment right now, so I’m slowing decisions down that I would normally just make and move on from. I keep second-guessing myself, especially with work things. I’ve had my assistant double-check vendor details more than usual, like prices or paperwork, because I’m worried I’ll miss something obvious or misunderstand something.\n\nAnd I’ve put off a couple of board items too, not because I don’t care about them, but because I just don’t feel steady enough in my thinking to handle them the way I normally would. So I’m functioning, yes, but it’s taking more effort, and I’m relying on extra checking and delaying things. That’s not really like me.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your current low and cloudy feelings are affecting your relationships with family and friends?\n\nPatient: With family and friends… I guess a little, but it’s not as obvious there as it is at work.\n\nI’m still going to work, so from the outside I probably look like I’m managing. But because I don’t trust my judgment right now, I’ve been slower about decisions, and that spills over into how I am with people. Like I’m less quick to say, “Yes, let’s do that,” or “No, that’s not a good idea.” I hesitate more. I second-guess myself.\n\nAt work it’s clearer because I’ve been asking my assistant to double-check vendor details, and I’ve put off a couple of board items because I just don’t feel confident in my thinking. With family or friends, it’s more that I’m not as decisive or as present, maybe. I don’t think I’m fighting with anyone or anything like that, but I’m quieter, more careful. I don’t want to say the wrong thing or make a decision and then regret it.\n\nSo yes, it affects them some, but I’d call it mild. I’m not cut off from people, but I’m not quite myself with them either.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your current low and cloudy feelings are making it difficult for you to find pleasure in almost all activities most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a fair way to put it. Maybe “almost all” sounds a little big, but honestly, the things that usually make me feel like myself, I haven’t wanted to do them.\n\nLike my dance class — normally I love that. Even if I’m tired, once I get there and the music starts, I feel better, I feel like I’m back in my body. Lately I just don’t want to go. I’ll think about getting dressed for it and it feels like too much, or like there’s no point.\n\nAnd I usually like looking at the little galleries, just wandering around and seeing what people are making. That used to lift me up. Now I think about it and I just feel flat, like I’d be going through the motions.\n\nEven cooking Sunday food for my family… that’s a big one for me. I’ve always liked doing that, planning the food, making enough for everyone, having the house smell right. Lately I don’t have the interest in it. It feels more like a chore than something warm.\n\nSo yes, I’m still doing some things, but the pleasure isn’t there the way it usually is. Those are the things that normally tell me I’m okay, and right now I don’t really want them.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’m not cut off from people, but I’m not quite myself with them either.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your current low and cloudy feelings are making it difficult for you to find pleasure in almost all activities most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a fair way to put it. Maybe “almost all” sounds a little big, but honestly, the things that usually make me feel like myself, I haven’t wanted to do them.\n\nLike my dance class — normally I love that. Even if I’m tired, once I get there and the music starts, I feel better, I feel like I’m back in my body. Lately I just don’t want to go. I’ll think about getting dressed for it and it feels like too much, or like there’s no point.\n\nAnd I usually like looking at the little galleries, just wandering around and seeing what people are making. That used to lift me up. Now I think about it and I just feel flat, like I’d be going through the motions.\n\nEven cooking Sunday food for my family… that’s a big one for me. It feels more like a chore than something warm.\n\nSo yes, I’m still doing some things, but the pleasure isn’t there the way it usually is. Those are the things that normally tell me I’m okay, and right now I don’t really want them.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say that your low and cloudy feelings are interfering with your daily life more than half of the time?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I would say more than half the time, but not in the sense that I’m completely stopped or can’t do anything. I’m still getting myself to work. I’m still showing up and doing the basic things people would expect me to do.

But it’s like everything takes more care than usual because I don’t quite trust my own judgment right now. Decisions that I normally would handle pretty quickly, I’m slowing down. I’ll look at something and then think, “Wait, am I seeing this right?” or “Am I missing something?” And then I don’t want to move too fast, because if I make a mistake, especially with work, other people are affected.

So I’ve been asking my assistant to double-check vendor details more than I usually would — things like paperwork, prices, little details that I normally feel confident handling. And I’ve put off a couple of board items too, not because they aren’t important, but because I just haven’t felt clear enough in my thinking to deal with them properly.

So yes, it is interfering more than half the time, I think. I’m functioning, but I’m functioning slower, with more hesitation, and needing more backup than I normally do.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because my head just feels… cloudy, I guess is the best way to say it. Now I’ll be staring at an email about something simple, like approving a vendor or deciding what to tell the board, and I just sit there. It sort of came on pretty sharply around when my husband retired, because all of a sudden he was home all day, every day, and the whole rhythm of the house changed.\n\nBefore that I was getting through things more like myself, but then there was this change in my energy and my confidence, and then it kind of dropped into this low, cloudy feeling. So… yes, about two weeks, not months and months, but these last couple weeks have felt much longer than they sound.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed since your husband retired and your household rhythm changed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad exactly. That’s actually one of the things that made me think maybe this isn’t just “being tired” or annoyed about the change at home.\n\nI usually go to my dance class, and it’s not like I’m a great dancer or anything, but I like the music and seeing the same people, moving around a little. Lately I just don’t want to go. I think about getting dressed and driving over there and I just… can’t make myself care enough.\n\nAnd I usually like looking at those little galleries, the small ones, just wandering in and seeing what people have made. That used to clear my head a bit. I still think about it, because it’s what I do, but the feeling behind it isn’t there. It’s like the things that usually bring me back to myself just don’t have the same pull right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any loss of interest in activities that you usually find pleasurable over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. I know I sort of touched on that already, but yes — the things I normally enjoy just haven’t been pulling me in.\n\nLike my dance class, for example. Usually I like going, even if I grumble a little getting ready. Once I’m there, the music and moving around and seeing familiar faces usually makes me feel more like myself. But lately I just don’t want to go at all. It feels like too much to get myself there, and even the thought of it doesn’t give me that little spark.\n\nAnd I usually enjoy looking at the little galleries, just wandering around and seeing what people are making. That’s normally something quiet that I do for myself. But these past couple weeks, I haven’t wanted to do that either.\n\nEven cooking Sunday food for my family — that’s a big one for me, because it’s usually something that makes me feel useful and connected and like myself. But lately I haven’t wanted to do it. It’s not that I suddenly hate these things. It’s more like the enjoyment has gone flat.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I haven’t weighed myself, so I can’t say for sure if the number has changed. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything like that.\n\nBut my eating has definitely been off. I’ll have coffee in the morning, and then maybe a few bites of something, and somehow that carries me through most of the day. Not in a healthy way, just like I can’t be bothered or nothing sounds worth making.\n\nThen at night I’ll start picking at things — tortillas, something sweet if it’s around — but I’m not even really enjoying it. It’s more like I’m standing there nibbling because it’s there, not because I’m hungry in a normal way. So I don’t know about weight, but my appetite and eating pattern are not like they usually are.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any trouble sleeping over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I don’t think I’ve been having some dramatic thing where I’m up all night every night, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSleep has maybe been a little… unsettled? But I also don’t always keep track well. Some nights I get into bed and I’m tired, and then I’m just lying there longer than I want to be, thinking about small things. Other nights I fall asleep okay enough. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a big clear pattern, though.\n\nI guess what I can say is I haven’t been waking up and thinking, “Oh, I only slept two hours,” or anything like that. It’s more that my sleep doesn’t feel especially refreshing, but I don’t know if that counts as trouble sleeping or just me feeling off in general.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or worthless about anything in particular over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. Not like I think I’m a terrible person all around, exactly, but I keep circling back to this thing with the residents.\n\nI spoke too big, I think. I said we could probably get certain things handled, and I sounded more sure than I should have before I really checked the money properly. And now I keep thinking, “Why did I do that? Why didn’t I slow down and look at the numbers first?” Nobody has come to me and said I ruined anything, and no one has accused me of anything like that, but in my head it feels like I let them down.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to even say, because maybe it’s not as big as I’m making it. But I keep replaying it, like I promised more than I should have, and now I feel foolish and guilty about it. Like people trusted me to know better, and I should have been more careful.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about death or dying over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I… yes, a little, and it scares me to say that out loud.\n\nIt hasn’t been like I’m planning something, or that I want to hurt myself. I don’t. I really don’t have a plan or anything like that. But a few mornings, when I first wake up and I’m still lying there, I’ve had this thought like… maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Like if I could just not have to face the day.\n\nAnd then I get scared of the thought, because I don’t want to die, not exactly. It’s more like I feel worn down in that moment, and my mind goes to that place before I can stop it. But I haven’t done anything to hurt myself, and I’m not thinking about a way to do it. It’s just been that thought, a few times, and it upset me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like you're speeding up in your thoughts or actions over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think both, which sounds odd when I say it.\n\nAt home, especially if I’m just going from one room to another or trying to get myself started on something, I feel slow. Like my legs are heavy, almost like I’m dragging myself around the house. It’s not that I can’t move, but everything takes more pushing than it should. I’ll stand there a second before doing something simple, like going to pick something up, and it feels like my body is behind me.\n\nBut then if I’m in a meeting, it’s different. I notice I’m tapping my pen, or moving in the chair, crossing and uncrossing my legs, shifting around. I’ll feel too keyed up to just sit still and listen normally. I don’t know if other people notice it as much as I do, but I notice it, and then I get self-conscious about it, which probably makes me move around more.\n\nSo yes, at home I feel slowed down, like everything is heavy, and around other people, especially in meetings, I can feel kind of wound up in my body.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your low, cloudy feeling and lack of interest in activities are interfering with your daily life and relationships a lot, or more mildly?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more mild than a complete breakdown, because I am still getting myself to work. I’m not just staying home or disappearing on people. But it is interfering, yes.\n\nThe main thing is I don’t trust my judgment the way I usually do. So decisions that I would normally just make, I’m slowing them down. I’ll sit with them longer, or I’ll say, “Let me look at that again,” even when maybe it doesn’t need that much looking. And I’ve been asking my assistant to double-check vendor details more than usual—things like whether I understood the price right, or if the paperwork matches what I thought it said. I’m grateful she can do that, but it makes me feel a little embarrassed, like I should be able to handle it without leaning on her so much.\n\nAnd I’ve put off a couple of board items, not because they’re impossible, but because I just don’t feel confident enough in my own thinking right now. I keep worrying I’ll miss something or say something too firmly and then regret it. So yes, I’m functioning, but I’m doing everything more carefully, more slowly, and I’m relying on other people to check me. It’s not ruining everything, but it’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your current feelings of being low and cloudy are significantly impacting your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, significantly might be a strong word if you mean I’m unable to function, because I am still getting up and going to work.
